MINISTRY OF PERSONNEL, P.G. & PENSIONS 
(Department of Personnel & Training) 

ORDER 

New Delhi, the 13th July, 1990 

S. O. 1909—In exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of section 5 read with section 6 
of the Delhi Special Police Establishment Act, 1946 (Act No. 25 of 1946) the Central Government, with 
the consent of the State Government of Andhra Pradesh [Vide Home (SC-A) Department S.D. Rt No. 
1057 dated 21-4-1990] hereby extends the powers and jurisdiction of the members of the Delhi Special 
Police Establishment to the whole of the State of Andhra Pradesh for investigation of the following cases 
which were registered under sections of Indian Penal Code (Act No. 45 of 1860) alleged to have been com¬ 
mitted by the employees of Central Government undertakings and private persons :— 

RC No, & Sections of Law Name of the accused 

1. RC No. 4/87-SIU(X), dt. 14-9-87 under section Shri Gauri Prasad Goenka, Chairman, M/s Duncon 

120-B Indian Penal Code read with 409, sections Agro Industries Ltd., 31 NS Road Calcutta and 
420, 467, 468 and 471, Indian Penal Code others cheated the United Bank of India, Calcutta, 

and substantive offences under sections 409, 

420, 467, 468 and 471, Indian Penal Code. 

2. • RC No. 1 (S)/89-SIU(X), dated 12-6-89 under As in above case, 

section 120-B read with section 420, Indian 
Penal Code and sections 420 Indian Penal Code. 

3. RC No. 6/87-SIU(X) dt. 7-10-1987 under Sri Gauri Prasad Goenka, Chairman, M/s. Duncon 

section 120-B, Indian Penal Code read with sec- Agro Industries Ltd., 31, NS Road, Calcutta-1 and 
tions 420, 467, 468 and 471, Indian Penal Code others cheated the United Bank of India, Guntur 
and Substantive Offences under sections 420, 

467, 468 and 471, Indian Penal Code, 

[No. 228/16/90-AVD.Ifo]

MINISTRY OF COMMERCE 
(Office of the Chief Controller of Imports & Exports) 

ORDER 

New Delhi, the 17th July, 1990 

S.O. 1940.—M|s. Cadila Laboratories Ltd., 294, 
GIDC Industrial Estate, Ankleshwar, Distt. Bharudi- 
393002 were granted an import licence No. PfD| 
2277173 dated 20-7-89 for Rs. 1,12,35,100!- 
(Rupees One crore twelve lakhs thirty five thousand 


and one hundted only) . for import of 32 mmu of 
Penicillin G Potassium First Crystal under GCA, 

The firm has applied for issue of Duplicate copy 
of Customs purposes copy of the above mentioned 
licence on the ground that the original Customs Pur¬ 
poses copy of the licence has been lost or misplaced. 
It has further been stated that the Customs purposes 
copy of the licence was registered with any Customs 
Authority, Bombay and as such the value of Customs 
Purpose copy has been utilised partially leaving a 
balance of Rs. 22,40,8031-. 

In support of their contention, the licensee has 
filed an affidavit on stamped paper duly sworn in be¬ 
fore a Executive Magistrate Ahmedabad. I am ac¬ 
cordingly statisfied that the original Customs Pur¬ 
poses copy of import licence No. P|D|2277173 dated 
20-7-89 has been lost or misplaced by the firm. In 
exercise of the powers conferred under sub-clause 
9(cc) of the Import (Control) Order, 1955 dated 
7-12-1955 as amended the said original Customs Pur¬ 
poses Copy No. P|D|2277173 dated 20-7-89 issued 
to Mis. Cadila Laboratories Ltd. is hereby cancelled. 

A duplicate Customs Purposes copy of the said 
licence is being issued to the party separately. 

[No. Suppl |NS-51650|DGTD|AM-901 SLS] 

S. KUJUR. Dy. Chief Controller of Imports & 

Exports

MINISTRY OF INDUSTRY 
(Department of Chemicals & Petrochemicals) 

New Delhi, the 11th July, 1990 

S.O, 1945,—Whereas by a Notification of Government of 
India in the Ministry of Industry (Department of Chemicals 
& Petrochemicals) No. S.O. 3183 dated the 1st December, 
1989 under sub-stetion (1) of Section 3 of the Petroleum 
& Minerals Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in Land) 
Act, 1962 (50 of 1962) the Central Government declared its 
intention to acquire the Right of User in the lands, specified 
in the schedule appended to that notification for the purpose 
to the Government. 

And whereas the Competent Authority has'Tinder Sub- 
Section (1) of Section 6 of the said Act submitted jeport 
to (he Government. 


Further the Central Government has after considering the 
said report, decided to acquire the right of user in the lands 
specified in the schedule appended to this notification. 

Now therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by Sub- 
Section (1) of Section 6 of the said Act the Central 
Government thereby declares that schedule appended to this 
notification is heiehy acquired for laying the pipe lines. 

And further, in exercise of the powers conferred by Sub- 
Section (4) of that section, the Central Government directs 
that the right of user in the suid lands shall instead of vesting 
in the Central Government, vest on the date of the publica¬ 
tion of this declaration in the Indian Petrochemicals Corpora¬ 
tion Limited, Maharashtra Gas Cracker Complex Division, 
Vile Parle, (W). Bombay free from all encumbrances.

New Delhi, the 11th July, 1990 

S.O. 1950.—Whereas it appears to the Central 
Government that it is necessary in the public interest 
that for the transport of petroleum, oil, natural gas, 
effluent or any mineral from village Bense Tahsil Pen 
District Raigad to village Agarsure Tahsil Alibag 
District Raigad in the State of Maharashtra, Pipe¬ 
lines should be laid through the agency of Indian 
Petrochemicals Corporation Limited, Maharashtra 
Gas Cracker Complex Division Vile Parle (W),, 
Bombay; 

And whereas, it appears to the Central Govern¬ 
ment that for the purpose of laying such pipelines 
it is necessary to acquire the right of user in the 
lands described in the schedule annexed hereto; 


Now, therefore in exercise of the powers conferred 
by Sub-Section (i) of Section 3 of the Petroleum and 
Minerals Pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in 
land) Act, 1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Govern¬ 
ment hereby notify their intention to acquire the 
right of user in the lands referred to in the schedule; 

Any person interested in the said lands having any 
objection for laying the pipelines through the said 
lands may prefer an objection within 21 days from the 
date of the notification, to the Competent Authority, 
Indian Petrochemicals Corporation Limited, Maha¬ 
rashtra Gas Cracker Complex Division, Nagothano, 
Tahsil Roha, District Raigad. 

And every person making such an objection shall 
state specifically whether he wishes to be heard in 
person or by a legal practitioner. 


SCHEDULE TO NOTIFICATION UNDER SECTION 3(1) OF THE PETROLEUM AND 
MINERALS PIPELINES (ACQUISITION OF RIGHT OF USER IN LAND) ACT, 1962

Govemmeni that it is neces-ary in the public interest 
that for the transpurt of petroleum, oil, natuial gas, 
effluent of any materia) from village Wawc, Tahsil 
Pen District Raigad to village Rawe Tahsil Pen 
District Raigad in the State of Maharashtra, Pipe¬

Petrochemicals Corporation Limited, Maharashtra 
Gas Cracker Complex Division Vile Parle ,'W), 
Bombay; 

And whereas, it appears to the Central Govern¬ 
ment that for the purpose of laying such pipelines, 
it is necessary to acquire the rigid of user in the lands 
described in the schedule annexed hereto; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred 
by Sub-Section (i) of Section 3 of the Petroleum and 


Minerals Pipe’ines (Acquisition of Right of User in 
land) Act, 1962 (50 of 1962). the Central Govern¬ 
ment hereby notify their intention to acquiie the 
right of user in the lands referi ed to in the schedule; 

Any person interested in trie said lands having 
any objection for laying the pipelines through the 
said lands may prefer an objection within 21 days 
from the date of the notification to the Competent 
Authority, Indian Petrochemicals Corporation 
Limited, Maharashtra Gas Cracker Complex Division, 
Nagothane, Tahsil Roha, District Raigad.

state specifically wheat tr he wisres to be heard in 
person or by a legal practitioner.

S.O. 1952.—Whereas it appears to the Central 
Government that it is necessary it: the public interest 
that for *he transport ol petroleum, oil, natural gas, 
effluent or any material from village Sai Tahsil 
Panvel, District Raigad to village Cavan, Tahsil 
Panvel, District Raigad in the State of Maharashtra, 
Pipelines should be laid through the agency o' Indian 
Petrochemicals Corporation Limited, Maharashtra 
Gas Cracker Complex Division Vile Parle (W), 
Bombay; 


And whereas, it appears to the Central Govern¬ 
ment that for the purpose of laying such pipelines, 
it is necessary :u acquire the right of user in the lands 
described in the schedule annexed hereto ; 


Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred 
by Sub-Section (i) of Section 3 of the Petroleum and 
Minerals Pipelines (Acquisit-'in of Righi of User in 
land) Act, 1962 (50 of 19f>2), the Central Govern¬ 
ment hereby notify their intention to acquire the 
right of user in the lands referred to in the schedule. 

Any person interested in the said lands having 
any objection for laying the pipelines through the 
said lands may prefer an objection within 21 days 
from the date of the notification, to the Competent 
Authority, Indian Petrochemicals Corporation 
Limited, Maharashtra Gas Cracker Complex Division, 
Nagothane, Tahsil Roha, District Raigad. 

And every nersnn making such an objection shall 
state specifically whether he wishes to be heard in 
person or by a legal practitioner

MINISTRY OF PETROLEUM AND CHEMICALS 
New Delhi, the 13th July, 1990 

S.O. 1959.--Whereas it appears to the Central Govern¬ 
ment that it is necessary in the public interest that for the 
transport of Petroleum and Natural Gas through Tatipaka- 
Kakiuada pipeline is to be laid by the Gas Authority of 
India Ltd. 

And, whereas, it appears that for the purpose of laying 
this pipeline it is necessary to acquire the right ol user in 
the land described in the schedule annexed hereto; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by 
Sub-Section (i) of the section 3 of the Petroleum <5L Mine¬ 
rals pipelines (Acquisition of Right of users in the land) 
Act, 1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Government hereby 
declares its intention to acquire the Right of user therein; 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 21 days from the date of this notification object to 
the laying of the pipeline under the land to the Compe¬ 
tent Authority. Gas Authority of India Ltd. K. O. Basin 
Project, 12|76, Prakash Nagar, Rajahmundry-533103, Andhra 
Pradesh. 

And every person making such an objection shall ulso 
state specifically whether he wishes to be heard in person 
or by legal Practitioner.

S.O. 1975.—Whereas it appears to the Central Govern¬ 
ment that it is necessary in the Public interest that for the 
transport of Petroleum and Natural Gas through Tatipaka- 
Kakinada pipeline is to be laid by the Gas Authority of 
India Ltd. 

And whereas it appears that for tho purpose of laying 
this pipeline it is necessary to acquire the right of user in 
the land described in the schedule annexed hereto; 

Nowj therefore, it exercise of the powers confirmed by 
Sub-Section (i) of the section 3 of the Petroleum & Minerals 
pipelines (Acquisition of Right of Users in the Land) Act, 
1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Government hereby declares 

its intention to acquire the Right of user therein : 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 21 days from the date of this notification object to 
the laying of the pipeline under the land to the Competent 
Authority. Gas Authority of India Ltd. K. G. Basin Project, 
12/76, Prakash Nagar; Rajamundry-533103; Andhra Pradesh; 
Pradesh. 

And every person making such an objection shall also 
state specifically whether he wishes to be heard in person 
or by legal Practitioner.

sub-Section (i) of section 3 of the Petroleum & Minerals

its intention to acquire the Right of user herein : 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may,

Authority, Gas Authority of India Ltd. K. G. Basin Project,

land described in the schedule annexed hereto;

sub-Section (i) of the section 3 of the Petroleum & Minerals 
pipelines (Acquisition of Right of users in the land) Act, 
1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Government hereby declares 
its intention to acquire the Right of user therein; 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may,

desh.

pipeline is to be laid by the Gas Authority of India Ltd. 

And, whereas, it appears that for the purpose of laying this 
pipeline it is necessary to acquire the right of user in the 
land described in the Schedule annexed hereto; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub¬ 
section (1) of the section 3 of the Petroleum & Minerals Pipe¬ 
lines (Acquisition of Right of Users in the Land) Act, 1962 
(50 of 1962), the Central Government hereby declares its 
intention to acquire the Right of user thorein; 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 21 days from the date of this notification object to the 
tuyiog of the pipeline under the land to the Competent Autho¬ 
rity, Gas Authority of India Ltd., K. O. Basin Project, 12/76, 
Prakash Nugar, Rojahmundry-533103, Andhra Pradesh. 

And every person making such an objection shall also 
state specifically whether he wishes to be heard in person or 
by legal Practitioner.

land described in the schedule annexed hereto;

section (i) of the section 3 of the Petroleum & Minerals pipe¬

S.O. 2016.—In pursuance of section 17 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 194/ (14 t;f 1947) the Central Government 
hereby publishes the following award of the Central Govt. 
Industrial Tribunal No. 2, Bombay as shown in the Amiex- 
ure in the industrial dispute between the employers in rela¬ 
tion to the management of New Majri Colliery M/s. Western 
Coalfields Ltd. and their workmen, which was received by 
the Central Government on 3-7-90. 


Employers in relation to the management of Ningha Col¬ 
liery of M/s. Eastern Coalfields Ltd,, P.O. Kalipahari 
District Burdwan. 

AND 

Their Workmen

ANNEXURE 

BEEORE T1IE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL 
TRIBUNAL NO. 2, BOMBAY 

Reference No. CG1T-2/15 of 1989 

PARIIES : 

Employers in relation to the management of New 
Majri Colliery t f M/s. Western Coalfields Ltd.

Their Workmen 

APPEARANCES : 


AWARD 

By Order No. l.-19012(72)/82-D.IV(B) dated 1st October, 
1984. the Government of India, Ministry of Labour & Reha¬ 
bilitation (Department ofLabour) referred the following dis¬ 
pute to this Tribunal for adjudication : 


For the Employers—Shri A. K. Sasi, Advocate. 

For the workmen—tl) Shri S, R. Pendre, General Sec¬ 
retary, L.B.K.K. Union. (2) Shri Ghryisham Bhagat, 
workmen in person. 

INDUSTRY : Collieries STATE : Maharashtra. 


“Whether the action of the management of Ningha Col¬ 
liery of M/s. Eastern Coalfields Ltd., P.O. Ningha 
(Kalipahari), District Burdwan in stopping tho woik 
of S/Shri Ram Bilas Singh and Metiur Rahaman, Pit 
Clerks, Gr. II w.e.l. 20-10-80 is justified? If not, to 
what relief the workmen arc entitled 7” 

2. When the case is called out today for hearing, Mr. B. N. 
Lain, Advocate appears for the management. Nobody appears 
lor the workmen. Mr. Lala files a petition signed by the officer 
of the management and the concerned two Workmen as 
mentioned in the reference stating therein that the workmen 
are not interested to proceed with the present reference. 
The present reference has arisen because of the industrial 
dispute raised by the union sponsoring the cause of the 
aforesaid two workmen. The dispute involved In the refe¬ 
rence relatccs to the stopping of the work of the two con¬ 
cerned workmen as Pit Clerk Gr. II. Such being the posi¬ 
tion and as the union having not filed the present peti¬ 
tion no effective action can be taken on the present petition. 

3. Tho fact is however that the union has not nppeared 
inspite of the issue if the notice thereby indicating that the 
union is not interested to proceed with the presen reference. 
In the circumstances there is no other alternative but to pass 
a “No Dispute Award” and accordingly a “No Dispute 
Award” is passed. 

This is my Award. 

Dated, Calcutta, 

The 20th June, 1990. 

SUKUMAR CHAKRAVARTY, Presiding Officer 
[No. L-19Q12(72)/82-D.IV(B)] 


Bombay, daled the 25th June 1990 
AWARD 

The Central Government by their Order No. L-22012- 
(1871/88-D.lVB dated 19-5-1989 have referred the follow¬ 
ing industrial Dispute to this Tribunal for adjudication under 
Section 10(l)(d) of the Industrial Disputes Act:— 

“Wheiher the action of the Sub-Area Manager, New 
Majiri Colliery of M/s. W.C. Ltd. in stopping 
from service Shri Ghanshyam Parshuram Bhagat 
w.e.f. 1-4-1985 is justified? If not, to what relief 
the workman concerned is entitled ?” 

2. The workman Shri G. P. Bhagat himself filed his 
statement of claim (Ex. 2) challenging the said action of 
the management. 

3. The management filed their written statement (Ex, 3) 
in support of their action. 

4. The necessary issues have been framed at Ex. 4, 

5. While the case was at the stage of evidence the period 
arrived at an amicable settlement and filed the Memo of 

settlement which is thus :—

stated on the same post as held by him at the time 
of termination.

The Government of India in the Ministry of Labour in 
exercise of the powe's conferred on them by clause (d) 
of sub-section (1) and sub-section (2A) of Section 10 of 
the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 has referred the dispute 
to this Tribunal for adjudication vide Ministry’s Order No.

SCHEDULE 

“Whether the action of the Management of Chinakuri 3 
Pits Colliery of M]s. E.C. Ltd., P.O. Sunderchak, 
i>ist. Burdwan in dismissing from service Sri Bharat 
Ghosh, Clerk is justified ? If not, to what relief 
the workman concerned is entitled V 

2. Sri Bharat Ghosh, the concerned workman was a Clerk 
of Pit No. 3 of Chinakuri Colliery under Eastern Coalfields 
Ltd. The allegation against him' in short is— 

‘That it was detected by the Management during the 
course of checking that Sri Bharat Ghosh, the clerk 
concerned prepared false bills—No, 87 dL. 23-5-1984 
for Rs. 1407.77 against the name of Sri Srinarayan 
Loco Driver, No. 7 dt. 5-10-1984 for Rs. J256.83 
against the name of Kati Manjhi, Driller and No, 
10 dt, 5-10-1984 for Rs. 267.34 against the name 
of Bodi Kora, Haulage Khalasi as unclaimed wages, 
even though, no such unclaimed wages weie credit¬ 
ed in the unpaid wages register of the colliery. He 
prepared all the above fictitious bills, withdrew the 
bills by putting fake L.T.Is, on the bills and mis¬ 
appropriated the amount pf Rs. 2931.94 belonging 
to the Eastern Coalfields Ltd.’’ 

For such allegation he was served w'ith a chargesheet 
which reads as follows :— 

“It has been detected during coarse of checking that 
you prepared false bills No. 87 dated 23-5-84 for

L|Driver, No. 7 dated 5-10-84 for Rs 1256.83 
against the name of Kati Majhce, Driller and No.

of Bodi Kora. M, Khalasi as unclaimed wages though 
no such wages was credited in the unpaid wage 
register of the colliery. 

You prepared all the above fictitious bills and withdrew 
these bills by putting fake LT.I. on the bills 
and misappropriated company’s money amounting 
to Rs. 2931.94. 

Your act amounting to misconduct U[s. 17(i)ta), (f) & 
(i) of the standing orders which reads as follows :— 

Sec. 17(i)(a),—Theft, fraud or dishonesty in connec¬ 
tion with employer’s business or property. 

Sec, 17(i)(f).—Habitual neglect of work. 

Sec. 17(i)(i).—Causing wilful damage to work in 
progress or to property of tho employer,”

nr found Bharat Ghosh guilty for two counts of the charges

THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JULY 28, 1990/SRAVANA 6 1912 [Part T1 Sec ?(»)] 


Ext. M-13 is Voucher No. 7 dated 5-10-84 for Rs- 1236-83 
for th e month of August ’84 in the name of Kati Majhu In 
this voucher also are some references from the unpaid wages 
hook F.xt. M-14. After comparing Ext. M-13|l & M-1J|4 
with the entries of Ext. M-14, 1 find that the entries of Ext 
M-13|l & M-1312 are false. In the written reply Sn Bharat 
Ghosh has stated that :—• 

‘‘I never prepared any fictitious bill of three persons as 
alleged.” 

He has stated that it v.u-. not his duty to prepare vouchers 
of unpaid wages hut due to dearth of clerks he was saddled 
with additional burden of preparing unclaimed vouchers. 

In cross-examination he was put the following questions and 
he gave the answer as noted under question (page 30 of 
the proceedings) :— 

“Q, Did you prepare the unclaimed vouchers No. 87 
dated 23-5-84 for Rs. 1407.77, Vouchers No. 7 dated 
5-10-84 for Rs. 1256.83 and Vouchers No, 10 of 
5-10-84 for Rs, 267.34. 

Ahs. Yes, T have prepared the above vouchers. 

Q, Whether amount mentioned in the above said 3 
vouchers are recorded in the unclaimed wage regis¬ 
ter as unclaimed amount 

Ans.—-Amount mentioned in Voucher No. 87 dated 
23-5-84 (Ext-1) and in Voucher No. 7 dated 
5-10-84 (Ext-3) is not deposited in the unclaimed 
wage register. But the amount mentioned in Vou¬ 
cher No. 10 dated 5-10-84 (Ext-5) is deposited in 
the unclaimed wages Register.” 

So it is clear that he prepared the vouchers Exts, M-1311 
and M-13 (2. Next it was put to him whether the amount 
mentioned in the above vouchers were recorded in the un¬ 
claimed wage register as unclaimed amount. In reply he 
has stated that the amount mentioned in Voucher No. 87 
dated 23-5-84 and in Voucher No. 7 dated 5-10-84 were 
not deposited in unclaimed wages register (page 30 of his 
statement). Sri Bharat Ghosh has specifically stated— 

“the amount of bill in Voucher No. 87 dated 23-5-84 
and Voucher No. 7 dated 5-10-84 were not deposit¬ 
ed in Unclaimed register.’' 

He has specifically answered to the question on that point 
<Question in page No. 29). His answer is in page No. 28 : 

‘‘I ascertained the amount of wages from the wage hills 
of Srinarayan and Kati Majhi for the month of 
April ’84 and August ’84. 

So from his answer it is clear that it was known to him 
before preparation of the vouchors that nothing was due to 
Srinarayan and Kati Majhi for those months. Sri Bharat 
Ghosh has stated that— 

“T did not pay the wages of Srinarayan, Loco Driver 
for the month of April ’84. I paid the wages of 
Srinarayan to some other worker due mistake 
(page 26 of his statement) ” 

According to his statement it appears that it was his duty 
to disburse the pay for the month of April ’84 and according 
to him he made a wrong payment to Some one in place of 
Srinarayan. He cannot say to whom he made such pay¬ 
ment for the month of April '84, He has further admitted 
that he recorded the payment in the pay book of Srinarayan. 
Then he was asked— 

"Then how you prepared the unclaimed voucher and 
the answer of Bharat Ghosh is (page 25) — 

“Srinarayan was on leave. Hie demanded payment 
after coming hack from leave. I prepared the 
bill of unclaimed wages of Srinarayan to make 
a show before him that his wnges was unclaimed 
and to hide that I wrongly paid his wages to 
someone-else.” 

He has admitlcd that he also signed Voucher No. 8.7 Ext. 


M-13 [ 1 and Voucher No. 7 Ext. M-13|2 (page 24 of his 
statement). So considering the statement of Bharat Ghosh 
it is clear that it was known to him that the wages for the 
month of April ’84 of Srinarayan was not unpaid. According 
to him he made a wrong payment. If his statement is 
believed then we find that without reporting the matter to 
his higher aulhority he prepared the fictitious hill in the 
name of Srinarayan as admitted by him. 

9. Next we arc to consider about the Voucher No. 7 in 
the name of Kati Majhi. It is also admitted from the 
statement of Bharat Ghosh himself that the said amount 
covered by Voucher No. 7 is not unpaid and he himself 
prepared the said voucher and also entered the same in the 
summary sheet Ext. M-13. So considering the materials on 
record and (he facts there cannot be any hesitation to hold 
that Bharat Ghosh prepared a fictitious hill in the name of 
Kati Majhi which is Voucher No. 7 Ext. M-13/2. 

10. Now comes the question whether any amount was 
paid against those two fictitious bills. From the face of 
the documents we find that the payments were made on 
those vouchers. In page 27 of his statement Su Bharat 
Ghosh has stated that— 

‘‘I paid the Voucher No. 7 dated 5-10-84 in respect of 
Kati Majhi,” 

But in page 21 of his statement he made a belated attempt 
to say that he had returned the money to the cashier as 
the amount was withdrawn in excess from the main cash. 
There is no document in support of such statement. So it 
cannot be believed that he returned the amount to Cashier as 
claimed by him. It must be held that payment was made 
against Voucher No. 7. 

11- There are three aspects in this case, Firstly whether 
fictitious bills wete prepared and whether any payment was 
made on such fictitious bills and whether Sri Bharat Ghosh 
misappropriated those amounts covered by the bills. We have 
already found that Bharat Ghosh prepared the fictitious bills 
and wc also find that payments were also made against those 
hills. 

12. Now comes the question Whether Sri Bharat Ghosh 
himself misappropriated the amounts covered by those 
vouchers. Tt is clear from the materials on record and the 
statement of Bharat Ghosh himself that nothing was due to 
Srinarayan and Kati Majhi as unpaid wages for which 
vouchers like Fxt. M-13 71 (Ext. 1 of Enquiry proceedings) 
and Ext. M-13/2 (F.xt, 3 of Enquiry proceedings) were pre¬ 
pared. As regards Voucher No, 7 Bharat Ghosh himself has 
stated that he made the payment. As regards Voucher No. 87 
he has given us a story that he had prepared a fictitious bill 
for a wrong payment, We find that payment was made against 
both the bills. There is no material on record to show that 
Srinarayan and Kati Majhi had taken these amounts as 
nothing was due to them. So It must be held that Bharat 
Ghosh himself had taken those amounts. After re-apprisal 
of the evidence on record. I find nothing to disagree with the 
findings of the learned Enquiry Officer. 

13 Now comes the question of punishment. Tn this case 
we fined that Sri Bharat Ghosh had fraudulently and dis¬ 
honestly prepared fictitious bills and mis-apprOpriate the 
money covered by those hills, 

14, Considering the natme of the offence and the facts and 
circumstances, T find that dismissal is the only punishment 
which is pvnorlionnte with the guilt of the concerned work¬ 
man. So 1 find nothing to interfere with the punishment 
awarded by the authority. 

15. In the result I find that the action of the manage¬ 
ment of Chinakuri Pit No, 3 Colliery in dismissing Sri Bharat 
Ghosh, Clerk from service is justified. He is not entitled to 
get any relief. 

This is my award. 

N K. SAHA. Presidng Officei

S.O. 2019.—In pursuance of Section 17 of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947), the Central Govern- 
merit hereby publishes the award of the Industrial Tri¬ 
bunal, Bhubaneswar as shown in the Annexure, in the 
industrial dispute between the employers in relation to 
the management of Bolani Ores Mine of SATL, Bolani, 
Distt. Keonjihcr (Orissa) and their workmen, which was 
received by the Central Government on 3-7-90. 

ANNEXURE 

INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL ORISSA. BHUBANESWAR 
industrial Dispute Case No. 22 of 1989 (CENTRAL) 
Date, Bhubaneswar, the 26th June, 1990 
BETWEFN: 

The Management of Bolani Ores Mines of SAIL, 
Bolani, Dist: Kconjhar.

(AND)

Workers Union (CJTU), At|P. O. Barbail, Dist: 
Kconjhar.

Sri SK. Mukherjee, Manager (Personnel).—-For the 
First Party-Management. 

None.-—For the Second Party-workmen, 

AWARD 

The Government of India in the Ministry of Labour, 
in exercise of the powers conferred upon them by clause 
(d) of sub-secfttin (1) and sub-sect(On (2A) of Section 
10 of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (14 of 1947) and 
by their order No. L-260II|6|89-TR(Mhc.) have referred 
the following dispute for adjudication by this Tribunal: — 

“Whether the demand of the Keonihar Mining Wor¬ 
kers Union (CITU), Barbil that Shri Panchu 
Kama and 24 others casual employees should be 
regularised in their emnloyfent by the Manage¬ 
ment of Bolani Ores Mines of SAIL, P. O. Bot- 
ani, Dist- Keonjhar, Orissa with equal wage* 



3434 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JULY 28, 1 990/SRAVANA 6 1912 [Part II— Sec. 3(il)] 


including all benefits at par with the correspond¬ 
ing categories of regular employees, is justified? 
If so, what relief are the workmen concerned 
entitled to?” 

1. In this proceeding, on behalf of the second party- 
workmen statement of claims have been filed and written 
statement has been filed to the same by the hirst Party- 
Management. It has been stated in the written statement 
filed by the First party that out of the 25 second party- 
workmen, except Nakul Kama, Dullav Behcra, Bidyut 
Roy and Dinesh Chandra Adhikari the rest have been 
regularised and there exists no dispute at present relating 
to them. So far as the four workmen named above arc 
concerned, it is stated that Dullav Bihera, Bidyut Roy 
and Dinesh Chandra Adhikari are not the employees of 
the First Party-company and as such, question of their 
regularisation in service by the company does not arise. 
Dullav Behera is stated to be a contractor, who prepares 
and supplies tea to the Officers of the company and is 
paid service charges. He sells tea to the Officers of the 
company and receives the price for the same from the 
Officers. Bidyut Roy and Dinesh Chandra Adhikari are 
sfiid to be the employees of the Welfare Committee which 
runs a Recreation centre in the locality. It runs with the 
grants given by the Management of the company, Emp¬ 
loyees, representatives are also in the said Welfare Com¬ 
mittee. It is stated that sinec Bidyut Roy and Dinesh 
Chandra Adhikari were employed by the Welfare Com¬ 
mittee they have no connection with the Management of 
the company. Nakul Karua is said to be a Casual Sweeper 
bit because be is suffering from tubercular meninngitics 
and is now under treatment he could not be considered 
for regularisation. 

3. in this proceeding, today, since none appeared for 
the second party-workmen, they were set expurtc and 
evidence on the side of the First Party-Management was 
recorded. 

4. Three witness were examined on behalf of the First 
Party-Management in this proceeding. 

M. W. 1, the Superintendent (Mines), Bolani Ores 
Mines stated that Dullav Behera, whose name is men¬ 
tioned at serial No. 21 of the list annexed to the state¬ 
ment of claims field by the second party m this proccer- 
ing, not is a casual mazdoor employed by the First Patty 
company but he is a contracoir engaged for preparing and 
supplying tea to the officers of the First Party for which 
he is paid service charge of Rs. 40O|- per month. Accord¬ 
ing to him, Sri Behera charges 25 paise for each cup of 
tea from the consumers. He proved Ext. 1, the order 
dated 2-9-88 issued by the Management of the company 
raising the service charges which was being paid to Sri 
Bihera from Rs. 350|- to Rs. 400|- per month. 

M W. 2. the Asst. Manager (Mines), Bolani Ores 
Mines stated that besides being the Asst. Manager of the 
Mines he is also the honorary General Secretary of the 
Welfare Committee constituted for the Mines employees- 
According to him, the employees’ representatives are also 
iiiembefs of the Welfare Committee which is running 
with the grant received from the company. The Welfare 
Committee maintains a recreation centre and it employees 
club boys for the said centre. He stated that the com¬ 
pany has nothing to do with such employment. 

M. W- 3, the Dy. Chief Personnel Manager, Bolani 
Ores Mines stated that out - of the 25 second party-work- 
man, 21 have already been regularised in their respective 
posts and out of the rest 4, Sweeper Nakul Karua is 
suffering from Tubercular mcninnsitics for which he has 
not yet been regularised but on being cured and being 
declared fit by the Medical Superintendment of the Bol- 
ari Hospital, his case for nytularisaation would be taken 
up. He proved fHe xerox copy of the medical certificate 
issued by the Medical Superintendent, gf Bol.ini,.Hospital 
on 28-3-90 with regard to' the illness of Nakul Karua, 
M. W- 3 further stated that (he rest three are hot emp¬ 
loyees of the company and as such, question gf their re-' 
gularisation in the service of the company does hot arise. 


5. Evidence of the witness examined for the Manage¬ 
ment stands uncontroverted and as such are accepted. 

In the circumstance, I would hold that 21 out of the 
25 workman involved in this reference having already 
been regularised, question of passing an award in respect 
of the demand for their regularisation does not arise. So 
far as the rest four are concerned, Dullav Behera, Bidyut 
Roy and Dinesh Chandra Adhikari not being the emp¬ 
loyee's of the First Party-Company are not entitled to be 
regularised in the services of the company. 

So Tar as the claim advanced in respect of Nakul Karua 
is concerned, it is proved that at present he is seriously 
ill and has not been declared fit by the Medical Superin¬ 
tendent but as stated by M. W. 3 after he would come 
round and would be declared fit by the Medical Superin¬ 
tendent his case for regularisation would be taken up. In 
the circumstance. I would direct that soon after the 
workman Sri Nakul Karua is declared fit he shuold be 
regularised in his post, in as much as, there is no other 
allegation made by the Management so far as he is con¬ 
cerned which would obstruct the process of his regularisa¬ 
tion. 

The reference is answered accordingly exparte.

pute between the employers in relation to the manage¬ 
ment of M|s. Overseas Clearing Corporation, Bombay 
and their workmen, which was received by the 
Central Government on 3-7-90.

AWARD PART-I 

The Central Government lias referred the following 
industrial dispute to this Tribunal for adjudication 
under Section 10 of the Industrial Dispute, Act, 
1947 : 

"Whether the action of the management of M|s. 
Overseas Clearing Corporation Bombay in 
dismissing their workman Shri P. S. Dr ego 
with effect from 31-12-1982 is justified? 
If not, to what relief the workman is entitl¬ 
ed ’’ 

This part of the award disposes of the preliminary 
issued whether the inquiry js vit'ated for any reason 
alleged by the workman. 

2. P. S, Drcgo (hereafter for short ‘the woikmen’) 
was admittedly in the employ of M|s. Overseas Clear¬ 
ing Corporation, Bombay, (hereafter for short, ‘the 
Management’) from 21-11-1973. According to the 
workman, he was working as ‘Clcrk-cum-Typist’, the 
management assert that he was only a Typist. It appears 
that originally the Management terminated his ser¬ 
vice with effect from 1-10-1977 by issuing him a letter 
dated 30-9-1977, without holding any inquiry or giving 
any notice. On a reference by the Central Govern¬ 
ment under section 10 of the Act, the Industrial Tri¬ 
bunal No. 2, Bombay, ordered his reinstatement with 
half back wages. Both the Management and the 
workman hied writ petition in the High 
Court—the former for setting aside the award 
in its entirety and the latter for full hack wages. The 
High Court dismissed the Management’s petition in 
limine and granted the workman’s petition for full 
back wages. As a result the Workman came to be 
reinstated with full back wages. 

3. After the reinstatement of the workman, the 
Management again served a charge sheet dated 
24-3-1982 on him. The total number of charges is 
8. The main charges were 

(a) habitual absence without prior permi ston 
on a number of davs from 113-7-1981 to 
19-3-1982. 

(b) refusal to accept about 27 letters tendered 
personally to the workman. 

(c) rude behaviour with responsible officers and 
other co-employees. 

One Shri A. K, Khan who is a practising Advocate 
was appointed Enquiry Officer. Assistant Manager 
G. R. Kadam, a retired Government Servant, was the 
Presenting Officer. The inquiry started on 17-4-82. 
In all 59 documents were put on record and oral 
evidence of 9 witne sses including Shri Kadam record¬ 
ed. The Enquiry Officer submitted bis report dated 
4-12-1982 to the Management, bolding all the eight 
charges duly proved. Thereafter the Management 
dismised the workman from their service with effect 
from 31-12-1982. These facts are not in dispute. 

4. The workman has challenged the validity of the 
inquiry on several counts. Shortly stated, these are : 

(1) He has angered the Management by his 
Trade Union activities and by taking the 
matter of his previous dismissal to the High 
Court. The action of the Management 
amounts to deliberate victimisation 
1859 GI/90—-19. 


loan/mnw o, iaia 3435 


(2) The inquiry is vitiated by gross breaches iff 
the principle, of natural justice, the parti¬ 
culars in (his regard being, 

(A) Enquiry Officer Khan and Presenting 
Oflicer Kadam had joined hands with the 
Management in seeing that the workman 
was dismissed from service by hook or 
crook; 

(B) The Enquiry Officer gro slv erred in re¬ 
fusing the Workman’s request to be ic- 
presented by an Advocate, considering 
the special circumstances that the Enquiry 
Officer himself was a practising Advocate 
and the Pre enting Officer, an experienced 
retired Government servant. 

(C) The enquiry was proceeded with, despite 
the Enquiry Officer’s knowing 1 hat the 
Workman was not paid subsistence allow¬ 
ance. 

(D) The enquiry was concluded with undue 
haste, resulting in denial of reasonable 
opportunity to tbc workman to defend 
lumself. 

(3) The workman has been awarded punish¬ 
ment twice on some charges. 

(4) Copy of the findings in the inquiry was not 
furnished to the Workman. 

(5) The original file of the inquiry proceedings 
ha., been tempered with. 

5. The Management deny the Workman’s contention 
that the inquiry is vitiated by any factor. According 
to them, he was given more than fair opportunity to 
effectively defend himself. They admit that the En¬ 
quiry Officer had declined the Workman’s request for 
being represented by an Advocate. However in this 
confection they point out that the workman bad no 
absolute right as such to claim the assistance of an 
Advocate and that he himself was well versed with En- 
qu : ry Procedures. The Management deny that any of 
the lapses attributed to them amounts to violation of 
the principles of natural justice. At any rate, he is not 
a victim of actual prejudice. They deny other . ad¬ 
verse allegations. The Management urge the Tribu¬ 
nal to uphold,the validity of the inquiry. In the alter¬ 
native, they have reserved their right to lead evidence 
before the Tribunal on merits in base it comes to 
contrary decision. 

6. The Management have examined Enquiry Offi¬ 
cer Khan, while the workman has filed his affidavit in 
support of hi case. He has been cross examined by 
Shri Deshpandc, learned counsel for the Management. 
The evidence as well as arguments were concluded 
before my learned predecessor Shri Justice Jamdar, 
who retired in the second week of November 1989, 
T have heard arguments of both rides afresh. The 
notification of my appointment as Presiding Officer 
of the Tribunal took some time. And now for two 
months or so, T have had no stenographic assistance. 
Hence the delay in making this award, which I verv 
much regret, 

7- dispose of the Workman's allocations 

which in my opinion arc not borne out by the mate¬ 
rials on the record. These are listed in para 4 supra 



3436 


THE G AZE TTE OF INDIA ; JULY 28, 1990/SRAVANA 6, 1912 LPart II-Sec. 3(u)J 


as items (1), (2A>, (4) and (5). On item (5), his 
case is that certain short signatures purporting to be 
his, are not in fact made by him, implying thereby 
that they have been forged. Wc have the evidence 
of the workman on oath that the initials on tlio en¬ 
quiry proceedings marked by my learned predecessor 
as A-l to A-5 are not made by him. As aganst 
this, Shri Khan asserts that the workman has himself 
made those initials after going through the proceed¬ 
ing; of the relevant days. In cross-examination the 
Workman has conceded that the contents of almost 
all the endorsement? ostensibly authenticated by the 
disputed signatures are correct, although he maintains 
that the contents were not written in his presence. It 
is difficult to appreciate what possible motive the 
Enquiry Officer or the Presenting Officer could have 
"had in inviting the obviously grave responsibility of 
forging the workman’s initials, when the content; of 
the endorsements sought to be authenticated by those 
initials, were admittedly true. I see no reason to 
prefer the bald word of the workman ro that of the 
Enquiry Officer. Accordingly T hold that the Work¬ 
man lias failed to prove his allegation regarding for¬ 
gery. 

8. The grievance of bias made a.eamst Die Enquiry 
Officer has no sub.slanec in it. Indeed a peru'al of 
the enquiry proceedings shows that by and large, he 
has been liberal to the workman. There is no subs¬ 
tance in the dhgalion that the Enquiry Officer and 
the Presenting Officer had joined hand in liquidating 
the workman’s career by hook or crook. Simply be¬ 
cause the workman was a Trade Union activist or 
that the Management had previously initiated pro¬ 
ceedings against him, and lost in the High Court, 
it does not necessarily follow that they were bent on 
victimising him. I also do not think that there is any 
substance in the workman’s contention 
that he was entitled to get a ccpv of the 
FnquitV Officer's findings before final orders were, 
passed against him. PE has not shown that there 
arc any rules to this effect, applicable to him. 

9. l find that the workman has failed to substan¬ 
tiate his objections summarised at items (1)., (2A), 
(4) and (5) in para 4 supra 

10. The Workman however, stands on firm 
ground, so (nr as his challenge on three oilier 
grounds geos. These are ; (1) that the Enquiry 
Officer ought to have allowed him to be represented 
by an Advocate, (2) that he ought to have adjourn¬ 
ed the proceedings beyond 12-11-82 and (3) that he 
ought to have directed the Management to pay lum 
subsistance allowance as per Rules. T am further 
satisfied that failure to do tile needful on each of 
these three counts amounts to breach of the princi¬ 
ples of natural justice and has resulted in actual 
prejudice to the workman in each item. I proceed 
to state my reasons in short. 

11. First the question relating to the Workman’s 

right to be represented by an Advocate in the en¬ 
quiry proceedings. Tire legal position is by no- 
more or less well settled. As observed by a Division 
Bench of the Bombay High Coup ' in 1986 
1 743 A.B Furtado Vs. Chairman and 

Managing Director. Bank of India, Bombay and 
others, although ordinarily Courts should dis¬ 


courage the. involvement o £ lega' practitioners in 
domestic inquiries in order to avoid delay and com¬ 
plexities, nevertheless, the Court cannot ignore that 
tn exceptional cases representation by a lawyer is 
necessary, for otherwise there may be failure of tbo 
inquiry itself and a denial of proper and effective 
defence. A number of situations in which denial of 
such opportunity may well amount to infraction of 
the prin-.-pies of nfund just: j arc recognised by 
judical cuonomccmuits. 1 would life to mention 
some of them ns illustrations. 

(1) Where the charge against the Workman 
may be serious in nature, which, if prov¬ 
ed, may invite evil consequences for him, 
such loss of reputation or means of liveli¬ 
hood. Vide Furtado’s case, where the 
charge against the Workman was of mis¬ 
appropriation and fraud in respect of Bank 
funds. Advocate's assistance was held 
to be necessary, despite thy fact that 
neither the Enquiry Officer ncr the Pre¬ 
senting Officer was an Advocate or other¬ 
wise trained in Law. 

(2) Where a Workman is pitted against u legally 
trained mind, he will be entitled to be re¬ 
presented by an Advocate although there 
may not be any provision to that effect in 
the standing orders or Rules applicable to 
to nitn ; vide 1983 1 L.L.N. 314 Board o? 
Trustees of the Port of Bombay vs, Difip- 
kumar (S.C.J, 1984 II L.L.M. 294 Ghatge 
Patil Transport (Pvt.., vs. B.K. Etale 
and others. In the latter case, the Person¬ 
nel Officer of the Management was the 
Presenting Officer. It was held by the 
Bombay High Court that in view of this 
circumstance, the Workman was entitled 
to be represented by an Advocate and that 
denial of this facility to him amounted to 
denial of reasonable and fair opportunity 
to the workman to defend himself, thereby 
vitiating the inquiry altogether. 

(3) In 1985 11 L.L.N. 690 Union of India Vs. 
Karunakaran Nair, the Presenting Officer 
was an Inspector of the Central Bureau of 
Investigation. The workman was given 
the facility to be represented by a collea¬ 
gue, but not by an Advocate. It was 
held that although the Presenting Officer 
was not an Advocate or a Graduate in 
Law, being well-versed in the techniques 
of disciplinary enquiry, he was more than 
an Advocate, and us such the workman 
was entitled U> the- assistance of 3n Advo¬ 
cate. The denial of the facility to him re¬ 
sulted in breach of principles of natural 
justice, serious enough to vitiate the inquary. 

12. The question whether the chargesheetcd 
workman is entitled to the assistance of an Advo¬ 
cate. will depend upon the facts and circumstances 
of the particular case. In the present ease, it is 
not in dispute that Enquiry Office! Khan was a 
pradtisilngj Advocate of about 7 years standing in 
the Bombay City Civ'd Cmut at the material time in, 
1982. (See first pa r a of his deposition'!. It has also

come in his evidence that Shri Kadam who was the 
Presenting Officer was a retired Government servant. 
As this Kudum is no more, the Management, could 
not examine him before the Tiibuna!. But that does 
not make any difference. On going through the 
proceedings of the inquiry, I do find that he was 
Weil trained and experienced in the techniques of 
disciplinary pioceedings. I ant aware that the Work¬ 
man is also a Trade union activist. Even so, 1 can 
not help feeling that he was no match for Shri Kadam 
who had rich experience as Government servant. 

13. There is one more aspect relevant in Ibis re¬ 
gard. It is the case of the Workman that of the 
eight heads on which he was chargesheeied in the 
present inquiry, on three counts of alleged mis¬ 
conduct he has already been punished. These are : 
Charge No. J-relating in part to his absence on 
23-7-1981 and 30-7-1031; Charge No. 3 relating to 
his alleged rude behaviour towards Shti Ktdam (the 
same person as subsequent!/ worked as Presenting 
Officer in the enquiry pioceedings) on 1 -3-1982, 
and Charge No. 7 relating to his misbehaviour with 
the Management firm’s partner Parab. The work¬ 
man relies on certain documents in this regard : 
Vide Management’s letters dated 14-8-1981 
1-3-1982 and 13-3-J982 at pages 23. 39 and 44 if 
File No. 5 in the departmental proceedings. He 
submits that the Management were not justified in 
including these charges again in the present mquiry. 

J do not th'nk T should finally decide the question 
of double punishment at this stage. It however can 
not be denied that the question did involve conside¬ 
ration of a legal pica for which a ready answer was 
not available. At lease so far as his absence on 
23rd and 30th July, 198! is concerned, the 
Management’s letter on 14-8-81 does show that a 
severe warning was administered to him, after re¬ 
jecting his explanation. Mere reference may be 
made to a decision oi the Madras High Court in 
1969 L.I.C. 38 K. Anantha Narayan Vs. State of 
Madras holling that whereas department of the 
Government has punished an employee for one 
offence, other department of the same Government 
cannot again punish him for the same offence. The 
question raised by the Workman could not possibly 
have been effectively handled by him without Advo¬ 
cate’s assistance. There is substance in his conten¬ 
tion that he required services of an Advocate for 
effectively putting this aspect of his defence also 
before the Enquiry Officer. 

14. In the present cas? it is common ground that 
the workman had moved the Enquiry Officer for 
permitting him to engage an Advocate and that this 
application was made at the very threshold of the 
proceedings. Shri Khan states that he rejected this 
application for the only reason that in his opinion a 
workman had no right to engage an Advocate in a 
domestic inquiry under any circumstances. He has 
made it clear that this was the only ground for his 
rejecting the Workman’s reciucst. Needle's to say, 
the Enquiry Officer’s perception of the relevant law 
was not correct. Otherwise in all probability he 
might have granted the workman’s request. 

IT Taking into consideration all circumstances, 
including that both the Enquiry Officer and the 
Presenting Officer were of legally trained mind, and 


that the charges involved determination of serious 
questions of law and fact, I am satisfied that the 
Workman should have been allowed to engage an 
Advocate. Mo it of the charges were of serious 
nature. The Workman is alleged to have misbehaved 
himself with Shri Kadam, Assistant Manager, Shri 
Parab, Partner of the Management firm and other 
employees. Denial of the facility of engaging an 
Ajdvocate has actually worked prejudice to the 
Workman. Ibis factor alone vitiates the inquiry. 

16. Now, 1 fake up the question whether the 
Enquiry Officer should have granted adjournment to 
the Workman beyond 12-11-82. The i el event facts 
are mostly not in dispute. Immediately before 
12-11-82, the proceedings were fixed for 5-11-1982 
for defence statement of the Workman, He fell un¬ 
conscious on that day dur’ng the course of the 
proceedings. Arrangements were made by the 
Management to remove him to the St. George's 
Hospital. There he was given medic?.'I treatment. 
The workman regained consciousness and was allow¬ 
ed to go after some time. The inquiry proceedings 
were adjourned for the next day, that is 6th 
November 1982. The Doctor who treated the Work¬ 
man gave hirn a certificate to the effect that he 
needed 15 days’ rest. The Workman appeared before 
the Enquiry Officer on 6-11-1982, and admittedly 
presented the certificate before him and requested for 
two weeks adjournment. The Presenting Officer 
strongly opposed the request. Eventually the proceed¬ 
ings were adjourned for 12th November 1982. 

17. Now what transpired on 12th is important. On 
that day, the Enquiry Officer took up the matter four 
times, waiting for the Workman to come. The pro¬ 
ceedings’ were called out at 11.00 a.m., 12.00 noon, 
2.00 p.m. and 3.30 p.m. The. workman did not. turn 
up. Shri Kadam presented two letters to the Enquiry 
Officer, strongly opposing further grant of time to the 
Workman. Thees are at Exh. 59 and 60 in the en¬ 
quiry proceedings. Ultimately the Enquiry Officer 
closed the proceed-ngs around 4.45 p.m. (See Shri 
Khan’s cross-examination para 14). Tt is the Work¬ 
man’s grievance that he had addressed two letters to 
the Enquiry- Officer on 4th and 10th November 1982 
to the office address of the Management. Out of 
these, the latter one was for adjourning the matter to 
20th November- Nmv curiously enough, neither of 
the e letters which were sent by registered nost came 
to be de five red to the Enquiry Officer. They were 
returned back to the Workman in December. The 
letters bear a number of postman’s endorsements of 
various dales in November, including ffic crucial date 
12-11-82 on which the enouirv proceedings were finally 
closed—to the effect ‘N.F.fi meaning ‘Not found’. Tt 
is really perplexing (hat the Postman ddi not soot the 
Enquiry Officer in th e Management’s office on 12tfi, 
although according to him. he was present there 
throughout the day from 11.00 a.m. to 4.45 n m. It 
is stifi more confounding as to wbv the Management 
did nol collect these two Tetters f 1 ^ and arrange to 
hand them over to the •At-n-bv Officer, as they wed 
in dn formerly, khri Khan has oamn ; n pj R dc-'osi- 
lion that throughout the dm- on 12-1 1-82 he had kept 
on inouirinir from the employees of the M,-increment 
whether they had received unv communication from 
the workman, and each time he received a reply in the 



3436 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : JULY 28, iy90/SRAVANA 6 , 1912 [Part 1I--Slc\ 3(ii)J 


negative. I think there is substance in the contention 
of the workman that the Management had manoeuvred 
to see that the two leltetn of the workman did not 
reach the Enquiry Officer on 12-11-1982, 

18. It is significant to note that even in May 1981 
die Workman had gone unconscious durig the course 
of the inquiry proceedings. Shri Khan has admitted 
this fact in his deposition. The Doctor who examined 
and treated the Workman on 12-11-82 had recommen 
ded two weeks rest. The Enquiry Officer hac 
personally seen the certificate on 0-11-1982. In the 
circumstances lie was not justified in giving an unduly 
short adjournment. It is true that the inquiry had 
extended over 57 dates—as poined out by the 
Management’s learned Counsel. But the number of 
these previous adjournments did not justify refusal of 
adjournment beyond 12-11-82, when it was unavoid¬ 
ably necessary. We have to note that the inquiry was 
at a crucial stage—the workman’s defence statement 
was being recorded. The Enquiry Officer’s refusal to 
grant adjournment beyond 12-11-82 had deprived the 
Workman of opportunity to put up his defence, This 
has obviously worked in prejudice to him. 


lit. This takes me to the question as to the effect 
ot nonpayment ot subsistence allowance of the Work¬ 
man according fir Rules. The Workman’s claim is 
not disputed that he was entitled to full wages as 
subsistence allowance, whereas he wa s paid only half. 
The Enquiry Officer has admitted in para 10 of his 
cross-examination that the Workman had filed an 
application before him for a direction to the Manage¬ 
ment to pay suspension allowance to him, and that 
he did not pass any orders on the application. The 
Workman’s learned Counsel relies upon 1986 Lab. 
T.C. 879 Fakirbhai vs. Presiding Officer and another. 
The ratio of the case has been correctly Summarised 
under the first head note as follows : 


“The denial of payment of subsistance allowance 
to a workman placed under suspension, dur¬ 
ing the pendency of the proceeding under 
S.33(3) amounts to violation of principles 
of natural justice. Since it is difficult to 
anticipate the result of the application made 
before the Tribunal it is reasonable to bold 
that the workman against whom the applica¬ 
tion is made should be paid some amount 
by way of subsistence allowance to enable 
him to maintain himself and members of 
bis family and also to meet the expenses 
of the litigation before the Tribunal. And 
if no amount is paid during the pendency 
of such an application it has to he held 
that the workman concerned has been 
denied a reasonable opportunity to defend 
himself in the. proceedings before the Tri¬ 
bunal. Such denial leads to violation of 
principles of natural justice and consequen¬ 
tly vitiates the proceedings before the Tri¬ 
bunal under sub-section (3) of section 33 
of the Act and any decision given in those 
proceedings against the wor km an concer¬ 
ned.” 


Shii Deshpande for the Management seeks to dis¬ 
tinguish tne ease on die giound that it arose out of 
a matter under section 33(3) of the Industrial Dis¬ 
putes Act, whereas the present is a matter arising 
out ot departincutal proceedings. In my humble 
opinion the distinction sought to be made is without 
difference. The main reason why tho Supreme 
Court held that some subsistence allowance oughl 
to be- paid to the Workman during the pendency of 
the section 33 proceedings was that the workman 
should be in a position to maintain himself and his 
dependants, and to meet the expenses of the litiga¬ 
tion. All these considerations hold good even dur¬ 
ing the pendency of disciplinary proceedings. As 
it is, it is not shown that the present workman had 
sufficient alternate means to meet the aforesaid ex¬ 
penses. In these days of sky rocketing prices, the 
pinch caused by lack of funds is all the more sling¬ 
ing. Without dilating further, I hold that the failure 
of the Enquiry Officer to give any direction to the 
Management to pay subsistence allowance to-the 
Workman, is violative of the principles of natural 
justice and has resulted in actual prejudice to the 
workman in the present case. 

20. It cannot be denied that as a result of the 
three lapses on the part of the Inquiry Officer—dec¬ 
lining the Workman’s requests for permitting him to 
engage an Advocate, for adjourning the matter beyond 
12-11-82 and for directing the Management to pay 
subsistence allowance to him in accordance with the 
Rules—-the workman stood deprived of a reasonable 
and fair opportunity to defend himself. As a result, 
the enquiry proceedings stand vitiated. The Manage¬ 
ment have requested for opportunity to lead evidence 
before the Tribunal on the charges levelled against the 
Workman. The Management shall have the liberty 
to lead such evidence before the Tribunal, as they 
deem fit to substantiate their charges against the 
Workman. The Management shall have the liberty 
evidence in rebuttal. Parties to appear before the 
Tribunal on 3-8-1990 for directions. Costs to abide 
the final result. 

S, N, KHATRI. Presiding Officer

AWARD 

The Central Government by their order No, L- 
12012'612l87-D.il(A), dated 17-6-1988 have referr¬ 
ed the following industrial dispute to this Tribunal 
for adjudication under Section 10(1 )(d) of the Indus¬ 
trial Disputes Act :— 

“Whether the action of the management of 
Union Bank of India in terminating the 
services of Shri Y. G. Patil, an armed watch¬ 
man while he was on probation was justi¬ 
fied ? If not, to what relief the concerned 
workman is entitled ?" 

2, The case of the workman Shri Y. G. Patil as 
di closed from the statement of claim (Exh-2) filed 
by him, in short, is thus ;■— 

The said workman was appointed as an Armed 
Watchman on 21-7-1986 by the Un : on Bank 
of India, Margao Branch, Since then he 
was continuously working without anv 
break in servide and without anv complaint 
from anybody till the termination of his 



[urn II -wi :HU)] ' T' 71 * 

service on 10-4-1987. While in service he 
worked deligently and efficiently, and did 
not give any scope for any complaint 
against him On a certain occasion, u cer¬ 
tain customer had left a sum of Rs. 5,000 
on the counter and left the premises. The 
workman honestly handed over that amount 
to the Branch Manager, who later on handed 
it over to the said Customer, and on one 
more occasion the workman had found a 
sum of Rs. 10,000 left by some other custo¬ 
mer ,n the Bank premises. The workman 
again handed over that amount to the Branch 
Manager, which was later on handed over 
to the customer concerned. On a third 
occasion, the workman had found a turn 
of Rs. 100 in the Bank premises. He hand¬ 
ed over that amount to the Branch Manager 
who deposited it in some fund. In sp'le 
of his honesty and sincere service in the 
Bank it seems that the Branch Manager, 
Mapuca, made a complaint to the Zonal 
Office, Pune, eventhough the Branch Mana¬ 
ger, Mapuca was not at all concerned with 
him. On receipt of that complaint the 
Zonal Office issued a Memo, dated 
31-10-1986 to the workman advising him 
to improve his work performance, failing 
which his service would be terminated. 
Thereafter he was transferred to Ponda 
Branch by Memo dt. 10-11-1986, and 
he reported to the Ponda Branch on 
20-11-1986. 

3. On the report of the Branch Manager, Ponda, 
the services of the workman were terminated by the 
Manager, Department of Personnel, Western Zone II, 
Bombay. Therefore, he raised an industrial dispute 
before the Assistant Labour Commissioner (C) Vas- 
co-de-Gama. As the conciliation proceedings ended 
in failure, the Central Government made the refe¬ 
rence as above. The workman further alleged that 
the termination of his service is mala fide illegal, 
and unjust. The Bank officer had prejudice against 
him. The Branch Manager Mapuca had made the 
complaint against him. However, in fact he hud 
never worked at Mapuca branch, and as such the 
Branch Manager, Mapuca, had no right to make 
complaint against him at any time. The workman 
was directed to improve his work by the Memo, dated 
31-10-1986 issued by the Zonal Office, Bombay. 
However, he was not given any sufficient opportunity 
to improve the work performande, and he was im¬ 
mediately transferred to Ponda Branch At Ponda 
Branch he had hardly worked for 15 days. His 
services were termination on the basis of some como- 
lainf bv the Branch Manager at Ponda Branch. The 
workman was not given onnortunity to work during 
the nrohationarv period. He was transferred from 
one branch to anofhe-- so his work performance could 
not he assessed pronerlv by anv of the Branch Mana¬ 
gers, Under the Banking regulations, services of a 
probationer can he terminated at the end of the 
probationary period, if he is not considered fit for 
confirmation. However, in the present case the ser¬ 
vices of fhe workman were terminated before he had 
completed the probationary period. The services 


as, 199(1 /^“t b, 191 J 


of the probationer cannot be terminated during the 
probationary period except for misconduct, which is 
proved alter holding ilie necessary enquiry. However, 
the services of the workman were terminated for cer¬ 
tain alleged misconduct for which no enquiry was 
heid against him. As such Ms termination of service 
is illegal, unjust, and against the principles of natural 
justice. The workman therefore prayed for his re¬ 
instatement in service with full back wages and con¬ 
tinuity of service. 

4. The Manager (P) Western Zone 11 of the Union 
Bank of India by his written statement (Ex. 3) oppo¬ 
sed the said claim of the workman and in substance 
stated thus :— 

The workman was appointed as an Armed Watch¬ 
man on 21-7-1986 on probation and he 
wus posted at Margo Branch. As the work 
of the workman was found unsatisfactory, 
his services were terminated as per the 
provisions contained in para. 522(1) of the 
Sasiry Award, and the appointment letter 
issued to the workman by the Bank also 
slated that he was appointed on probation 
for a period of six months, and that during 
the period of probation his services were 
liable to toe terminated by gi\ing him one 
month's notice or one month’s pay in lieu 
of notice. 

As the services of the workman were found un¬ 
satisfactory, his service were terminated by 
giving him one month’s pay in lieu of 
notice, as per the provisions of Sastry 
Award and as per the terms of letter of ap¬ 
pointment. The Bank management admit¬ 
ted that the said workman had on a certain 
occasion handed over a sum of Rs. 5,000 
which was left on the counter by a certain 
customer, and also bad handed over Rs. 100 
to the Branch Manager on one more oc¬ 
casion. However, as regards the handing 
over of a sum of Rs. 10,000 by the work¬ 
man to the Branch Manager, the Bank had 
no record about it. However, the services 
of the workman were terminated because 
of his unsatisfactory work. Handing over 
of the amounts left in the Bank premises 
by the customers through over-sight is part 
of duty of an Armed Watchman. The 
Branch Manager, Margao Branch, had com¬ 
plaint against the woik performance of the 
workman m which though typographical er- 
for Mapuca Branch had been typed instead 
of Margo Branch. The Bank had issued 
a Memo, dated 31-10-1986 advising him to 
improve his work performance. The 
Branch Manager Margao Branch was asked 
to submit an appraisal report regarding the 
work and conduct of the wokman and ac¬ 
cordingly he had submitted his report, which 
stated that the work of the workman was 
lielow average, and that he was unfit for the 
service of the Bank. In spite of that letter, 
the Bank transferred the workman from 
Margo to Ponda Branch on 20-11-1986 with 
a view to give him a second opportunity to 
serve under another Branch Manager. 
However, the Branch Manager, Pyndu

5. The Issues framed at Ext. 4 are :—

Union Bank of India in terminating the ser¬

(1) Yes

(3) As per final order below. 

REASNS 

ISSUES NOS. 1 & 2 

7. Tre workman Shri Y. G. Patil field his affi¬ 
davit (Ex. 13) in support of his case. He was 
cross-examined on behalf of the Bank management. 
The Bank management examined three officers of 
the Bank in support of its case. It is an admitted 
fact that the workman was appointed as a Proba¬ 
tioner. Shri D. T. Nille (MW-1), Manager, Ponda 
Branch stated in his affidavit that the said workman 
Shri Y. G. Patil was working under him, that he 
had observed the work performance of the work¬ 
man and it was found unsatisfactory, that he warned' 
him from time to time, but noticed no improve¬ 
ment in his work. Then he submitted a confiden¬ 
tial report dated 12-12-1986 against the workman 
to the Regional office, Kolapur. Shri Francies 
Heredia, Manager, Margo Branch stated in his 
affidavit (Ex. 21) that the said workman Shri Patil 
was working as a Probationer in that branch, that 
his work was found not satisfactory, and that he 
submitted a confidential report dated 15-10-1986 
against him. Shri Harshad Ratilal Shah, Manager 
Department of Personnel, Western Zone-TI, Central 
Office, Bombay, filed his affidavit at Ex. 19. His 
office had terminated the service of the workman 
by the letter dated 31-12-1986. According to him, 
the service of the saij workman were terminated 
on the basis of the confidential reports received 
against him from the Branch Managers of Margao 
and Ponde. He further stated in his affidavit that 
in the letter of termination of service dated 
31-12-1986, through typing mistake Margao Branch 
was referred lo as Mapuca Branch, and that the 


Bank had thereafter informed the workman about 
the said mistake by its leter dated 27-1-1987. In 
his cross-examination the said witness (MW-2J 
stated that it is the Central office to terminate the 
services of any employe - , and hence the office at 
Bombay terminated the service of the workman 
validity and legally. Thus, according to the Branch 
Managers of Margao and Ponda, the work perform¬ 
ance of the said woikman was not satisfactory 
while he was working under them. 

8. Now, 1 wjlj deal with the doc time a I aiy evi- 
ence on record,. Ex, 5 is an order dated ] 1-6-1986 
issued by -‘he Bank management, under which the 
workman Shri Y. G. Patil was appointed assail 
Armed Guard. Clause 2 of this letter clearly stated 
that he was appointed on probation tor a period 
of six months after which period bis services would 
be confirmed if the work and conduct of the work¬ 
man were found satisfactory. Clause 3 of this 
letter stated that during the period of his probation 
his service is liable to be terminated by giving one 
month’s notice or by payment of one month’s 
wages in lieu of notice. Thus, as per the terms 
and conditions of his appointment letter, the ser¬ 
vices of the said workman were liable to be term¬ 
inated in case his. work was not found satisfactory, 
and his services were liable to be terminated during 
the period of probation by giving him on month’s 
pay. It is an admitted fact that the services of the 
said workman were terminated during the period 
of probation by giving him one month’s pay. While 
he was appointed as an Armed watchman on 
21-7-1986, his services wete terminated on 
10-1-1987 before the expiry of the probationary 
period. 

9. Ex. 6 is the confidential, report dated 
15-10-1986 issued by the Branch Manager of the 
Margao Branch (MW-3) Shri Francis Heredia. In 
this report, the said Blanch Manager clearly stated 
that the manner and conduct of the workman wer,e 
not satisfactory, courtsey and co-operation were 
saisractory, application to work was not satisfactory, 
and he was not having self confidence, while punc¬ 
tuality and regularity in attendances was regular. 
The gencraj remarks' were thus :— 

“Although Mr. Patil is regular in attciidcnce, 
his application to work, presence of mind 
and alertness is not at all satisfactory. 
Since Margao Branch is identified as 
vulnerable branch, it is not desirable to 
have such person as Armed Guard in our 
Bank. 

Wc therefore do not recommended that 
his services be confirmed and appropriate 
action be taken in this direction.” 

Ex, 7 K a Memo, dated 3J-K1-1986 (issued by the 
Bank officer to the workman, This Memo, stated 
that complaints were received against the workman 
Shri Patil that he was not applying his mind to 
work, and his aiartness and presence of mind were 
not satisfactory, and that an opportuniy was given 
<<> him to improve his work performance failing 
which he will be discharged. Thus, by this Memo, 
the workman was called upon to improve his work

letter of termination of services stated that as per

in his evidence that through typing mistake, the

ferred by a subsequent letter. As noted above 
in fact, the Branch Manager, Margao 0-, 9S6 

sent the confidential letter dated *

and that in fact the teference was to the confiden¬

is thus quite clear that as per the said confidential 
reports of Margao and Ponda Branch Manager. 
the work performance of the said workman was 
below average and was unsatisfactory. As per the 
terms of appointment (Ex. 5), the workman was 
to he confirmed after the probationary period only 
if his work was found satisfactory and that dm mg 
the period of probation his services were liable to 
be terminated by giving him one months, wages. 

Tl is an admitted fact that the strvices of the work¬ 
man were terminated during the probationary period 
by giving him one month’s pay. 

10 . It is seen that the services of the said work¬ 
man were terminated validly as per the provisions 
contained in para. 522(11 of the Shastfl Award 
which stated that the service of the probationer 
may be terminated with one month’s notice or one 
month’s pay and allowances in lieu of notice r l here- 
forc the contention of the workman that bis 
services could no bevalidly terminated during the 
period of probation, cannot be accepted. No mis¬ 
conduct is alleged against the said workman, mid 
as such there was no question of holding any 


iuan /wot fi. mi e 

departmental enquiry against him, as his services 
were terminated only because of teh uiisadsfactoiy 
work performance. 

11. In the case reported n J 971(1) 1 1 .1 between 
Dr. T. C. PiJlai and the Indian Institute of Techno¬ 
logy, Guindy, Madras, it was held by the Supreme 
Court thus :— 

“It is well settled that a probationer or a tem¬ 
porary servant can be discharged if it is 
found that he is not suitable for the post 
which he is holding. This can be done 
without complying with the provisions of 
Art. 311(2) unless the services are term¬ 
inated by way of punishment. Suitability 
does not deposed merely on the excellence 
or proficiency in work for confirming a 
person who is on probation. A particular 
attitude, or teridancv displayed by an emp¬ 
loyee can well influence the decision of 
the confirming authority while judging his 
suitability or fitness for confirmation,’’ 

Further, in the case reported in AIR 1963 Sup¬ 
reme Court 1552 between Rajendra Chandra 
Benerjec and the Union of India and another, it 
has been held by the Supreme Court thus :— 

“A government servant who is on probation 
can be dischaiged dining the period of pro¬ 
bation, and such discharge would not 
amount to dismissal or removal wjihiu 
the meaning of Art. 31.1(2) and would 
not attract the protection of that Article 
where the services of a probationer are 
terminated in accordance with the rules, 
not by way of punishment. A probationer 
has no right to the post held by him. and 
under the terms of his appointment he is 
liable to be discharged at any time during 
the period of his probation subject to the 
rules governing such cases." 

Therefore, as the work performance of the said work¬ 
man was not found satisfactory, the termination of 
his services during the period of probation cannot 
be held illegal, unjust and mala fide. According to the 
workman, he was not given sufficient opportunity to 
W'ork at Ponda Bianch. It is true that he had worked 
at Margao Branch for some period and thereafter he 
was transferred to Ponda Branch, and he had worked 
at Ponda for a short period. Eventhough he had work- 
cd for a short period, even for such short period his 
work performance was found not satisfactory by the 
respective branch managers. While the workman was 
appointed by the Zonal Office, Western Region Pune, 
his services were terminated by the West Zone-IT, 
Central Office, Bombay. According to the workman, 
the Central Office, Bombay, was not competent to 
terminate his services. The Manager, Department of 
Personnel, Western Zone, Central Office, Bombay, 
Shri H.R. Shah (MW-2) clearly stated in his cross- 
examination that it is the right of the Central Office to 
terminate the services of any employee and hence the 
office at Bombay term mated the services of the work¬ 
man, and that the Zonal Office is not competent to 
term inate the services of any employee. I, therefore, 
find that the Cential Office was competent to terminate 



3444 


THE GAZETTE OF TND1A : JULY 28, 1990/5RAVANA 6, 1912 [Pakt II— Sec. 3(ii)] 


in terminating the services of Shri Dhirajlul 
Chhabildas Sakaria, working as Peon in the 
Visavadar branch of (he Bank, without 
complying with the provisions of the J. D. 
Act, is just or not 7 

Advocate for the First Party—Shri J. M. Patel 
Advocate lor the Second Party—Shri B,B. Thesia 


the services of the workman. In the result, I find that 
the action of the management of Union Bank of India 
m terminating the services of Shri Y'.G, Patil, an armed 
Watchman, while he was 9* probation was quite just 
and proper. Issue No. 1 Js found in the affirmative. 
As such the workman is not entitled to any relief. 
Issue No. 2 is found accordingly. 

12. Even though the services of the said workman 
has been terminated because of his unsatisfactory 
work performance, still it is seen that he was a very 
honest workman, as on three occasions he had handed 
over certain amounts which were left by the customers 
through mistake on the Bank Counter, to the Branch 
Manager, It will be, therefore, just and proper that 
the Bank Management should consider him for 
appointment to some other post such as ordinary 
watchman or peon etc. According to tine said work¬ 
man he is an ex-serviceman. Therefore, the Bank 
management .houid consider his case sympatheti¬ 
cally. 

ISSUE NO. 3 

13. The following award is therefore passed. 

AWARD 

The action of the management of Union Bank of 
India in terminating the services of Shri Y.G. Patil 
an armed watchman while he was on probation is just 
and proper. 

The parties to bear their own costs of th/s Refe¬ 
rence. 

P. D. APSHANKAR, Presiding Officer

their workmen, which was received by the Central 
Government.

JUDGEMENT 

1- The present reference, under Section 10(1) (g) 
of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, which has here¬ 
inafter been referred to as the Act of 1947, has 
been entrusted to the Tribunal working at Ahmeda¬ 
bad, lor adjudication of the industrial dispute bet¬ 
ween the parties vide order No. L-i2012 640jS6-U. 
2(A) dated 8-7-8 7 by the Labour Secretariat of the 
Government of India, New Delhi. Since we are also 
working as Tribunal at Ahmedabad, this reference 
has been allotted to us for processing. 

2. The industrial dispute for adjudication in this 
reference is as under : 

“Whether the first party, Bank, has infringed 
any of the provisions of the Act of 1947, 
in terminating the services of Shri Dhirujlal 
Chhabildas Sakaria, working as a Peon m 
the Visavadar blanch of the Bank or not ? 
And if it has seen infringed, to what relief, 
the said workman is entitled to ?” 

3. The said workman has submitted the statement 
of demand at E\h. 5 and has stated that he had 
joined as a Penn at the Visavadar branch on 20-11-81 
and as such he was performing his duties with dili¬ 
gence and honesty. But he has not been continued 
in service after 31-8-82 and he has not been paid 
wages' lor all these days and has only been paid 
wages only of Sweeper for J4 days only at the rate 
of Rs. 7 50. As per the submission of the second 
party, workman, although he was appointed as i Peon 
he has not been paid the wages ot a Peon; but he has 
been paid wages ot a Sweeper only for 14 days; and 
when he was terminated, no notice was issued and 
no wages in lieu of the notice was paid or any re¬ 
trenchment compensation was pa’d. Therefore, his ter¬ 
mination from 1-9-82 has been an illegal step. Hence 
it should be decided that he continues in service and 
should be reinstated in service with full back wages. 

4. The first party. Bank, has opposed the said de¬ 
mand by submitting written reply as per Exh. 8. 
The submission of the Bank is such that when per¬ 
manent peon and sweeper of the Bank proceed on 
leave, ihe said workman was engaged for work on 
temporary basis. And accordingly the said workman 
was engaged as peon on temporary basis 29-1-82 and 
30-1-82 for two days and lor six days from 1-3-82 
to 6-3-82, because during that period, peon Shri 
Padia had proceeded on leave. Thereafter perma¬ 
nent Sweeper Smt- Barot proceeded oil leave and 
he was engaged for work as a Sweeper for eight 
flays from 15-3-82 to 22-3-82. Thereafter perma¬ 
nent Peon Shri Ptdia proceeded on leave from 
13-4-82 to 16-4-82 for lour days and for one day 
on 10-5 82, ns such he was engaged as a peon lor 
this one day. Thereafter Sweeper Smt. Barot pro-
